Comparisons between the trigger efficiency as predicted by Monte Carlo simulation
and as measured in data were performed for both the L1 and the
combined L1+HLT trigger chain, using
data recorded during 2018.
The trigger employed for those data is the combined
pufit
+
cell
algorithm with
thresholds as indicated in Table
2
.
The variant of the offline
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
used is that referred to as ‘tenacious’,
and is described in Section
4
.
The efficiency is measured using events containing a single muon,
which, as elsewhere, is treated as being invisible in the
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
calculation.
The selection requirements are otherwise similar to the row labelled ’VBF’ in Table
4
, except that:
the requirements on the VBF jets are changed such that
|\Delta\phi(\text{jj})|<2.0
and
m(\text{jj})
>
200
GeV
. In addition, for Figure
12(a)
, exactly two jets are required and
|\Delta\eta(\text{jj})|>5.0
, while Figure
12(b)
is binned in jet multiplicity and
|\Delta\eta(\text{jj})|>3.5
.
This selection is similar to that used in the VBF Higgs-to-invisible analysis
[
4
]
.

Monte Carlo simulated samples of the
Z\rightarrow\nu\nu
,
W\rightarrow\ell\nu
and
Z\rightarrow\ell\ell
processes
were generated at next-to-leading order (NLO) in strong coupling constant
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
using
Sherpa
2.2.1
[
30
]
.
These calculations use the Comix
[
31
]
and OpenLoops
[
32
]
matrix element generators, and merging was
done with the
Sherpa
parton shower
[
33
]
using the ME+PS@NLO prescription
[
34
]
.
The NNPDF3.0 parton distribution function (PDF) set
[
35
]
computed at next-to-next-to-leading order in
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
was used, along with dedicated parton shower tuning parameters developed for
Sherpa
2.2.1
[
36
]
.
After the events were generated, the response of the detector
[
37
]
is simulated using
\textsc{Geant}4

[
38
]
. Each

W
or
Z
boson event was overlaid with 15–70 pile-up collisions to match the distribution in data. The pile-up events were
simulated using
Pythia
8.1
[
39
]
with the MSTW2008 PDF set
[
35
]
and A3 set of parameters tuned to data
[
40
,
41
]
.
The efficiency as a function of offline
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
is shown
in Figure
12(a)
.
In the trigger turn-on region at lower values of offline
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
, a difference can be observed
between data and simulation, an effect largely attributable to the L1 trigger.
The lower panel shows the ratio of data-determined to simulated efficiency as a function of the offline
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
.
This ratio is an example of the scale factor that can be applied to simulated Monte Carlo events to correct
their
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
trigger efficiency.
Figure
12(b)
shows the efficiency with respect to
\langle\mu\rangle
for a selection that requires offline
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}>150\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and either exactly 2 or
\geq
3 jets.
It can be seen that the Monte Carlo simulations overestimate the efficiency by a few percent.
Such considerations show the need both to correct for the differences between data and simulation when working in the turn-on region, and to understand the behaviour of the resulting scale factors for appropriate selections.

6
Conclusion

Despite the considerable increase in luminosity during Run 2 of the LHC (2015–2018),
it was possible to maintain the excellent performance of the ATLAS
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
trigger.
This was achieved through a dedicated programme of developing, testing,
evaluating and optimizing various pile-up mitigation algorithms.

For triggers without any pile-up correction, a steep increase in trigger rate
with pile-up is observed.
This behaviour is consistent with expectations from a two-component
background
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
distribution model.

Several
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
trigger algorithms were introduced in ATLAS during LHC Run 2.
Both the first-level and high-level trigger algorithms were improved to maintain a similar level of efficiency throughout the
data-taking period.
These included a new high-level trigger algorithm which uses a fit to determine pile-up-induced local energy deposits
in individual events to reduce the impact of increasing luminosity on the
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
trigger rate.
In addition, it was found that combining algorithms related to different sources of high-
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
tails could,
with an appropriate choice of thresholds,
help maintain high efficiency while keeping trigger rates under control.
The new algorithms enabled the offline
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
selection to become efficient
about 25
GeV
earlier than would have previously been possible.
In the trigger turn-on region the new algorithms recovered up to double the number of events
that would have been possible previously.

A study of the
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
trigger performance for different signal samples shows only a small degradation of the efficiency despite the factor of four increase in instantaneous luminosity during the four-year LHC Run-2 period.
The
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
trigger behaviour agrees in general with predictions from Monte Carlo simulations.
However, the low-
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
region, where the trigger is not fully efficient, is more difficult to model precisely.
Since different
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
algorithms respond differently depending on the characteristics of the event,
it is necessary for analyses working in this low-
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
region to determine the specific
corrections appropriate to their particular event selection.

The predictions from the background model extend up to
\mu=200
,
the value anticipated for the HL-LHC
[
29
]
. The predicted rates are those that would be obtained if no
changes were made to the algorithm to mitigate pile-up.
The equal-threshold acceptance fractions predicted for this level of pile-up are about two orders of magnitude higher than in Run 2,
making that environment even more challenging for the
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
trigger.
The upgrades planned for ATLAS from 2021 to 2025 will
enable a factor of ten increase in trigger rate and a first-level trigger that takes advantage of the full calorimeter granularity.
As found in the results presented in this paper, mitigation of pile-up is possible,
by use of specifically-designed algorithms which can achieve lower rates for the same signal efficiency.
Use of tracking information can also mitigate the pile-up effect, as can increasing the cell noise thresholds.
However, from the rate predictions it is clear that further development of the high-level
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
trigger algorithms will also be required.

Appendix

Appendix A
Full definition of the trigger implementing local pile-up suppression

The details of the
pufit
algorithm, introduced in Section
3.5
, are as follows.

To estimate the contribution from high-
E_{\mathrm{T}}
energy deposits,
the calorimeter is divided into equal
area ‘patches’. Using 8 divisions in
\phi
and 14 in
\eta

yields a total of 112 patches, each with an area
similar to that of an
R=0.4
jet. The energy for each patch is computed from the
sum of all the clusters that fall within that patch. To prevent
a jet from being split into two low-
E_{\text{T}}
patches, four sets of patches are constructed
in parallel, with each set shifted by half of a patch size along
\eta
and/or
\phi
.
From the four candidate sets of patches,
the algorithm selects the set that yields the largest scalar sum of
E_{\mathrm{T}}
from the high-
E_{\mathrm{T}}
patches.
The transverse energy in each
patch is then used to select the hard-scatter and pile-up patches. Finally, a fit
is performed to determine the pile-up contribution to each hard-scatter patch.

The fit is constrained by two assumptions:
first that the vector sum of
\vec{E}_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{miss}}
over all pile-up contributions should be zero,
and second that the pile-up should be relatively evenly distributed throughout the detector.

Stage 1: selection of hard-scatter patches

The hard-scatter patch threshold is determined for each event from the trimmed mean
and variance of the distribution of the patch
E_{\text{T}}
values in the event.
The patch
E_{\text{T}}
values are sorted in ascending order
and a trimmed mean is determined by discarding the 5% of patches with the lowest
E_{\text{T}}
and the 5% of patches with the highest
E_{\mathrm{T}}
.
The trimmed mean is given by:

\langle\text{$E_{\text{T}}$ }\rangle_{\mathrm{patch}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=0.05N}^{0.95N}E_{{\textrm{T}}_{j}},

where
N
is the total number of patches.
The variance of the patch
E_{\text{T}}
values is estimated using the sample variance, calculated as

V_{\mathrm{patch}}=\frac{1}{N}\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{0.95N}\left(E_{{\textrm{T}}_{j}}-\langle E_{\textrm{T}}\rangle_{\mathrm{patch}}\right)^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{0.05N}\left(E_{{\textrm{T}}_{j}}-\langle E_{\textrm{T}}\rangle_{\mathrm{patch}}\right)^{2}\right\}\penalty\ ,

where the second term uses the lowest
5\%
of patches (already included in the first term) to estimate
the contribution to the variance from pile-up in the highest
5\%
. The highest 5% are excluded because the sample is biased by hard-scatter jets.
The patch threshold is then set to

E_{\textrm{T}}^{\mathrm{thres}}=\langle E_{\textrm{T}}\rangle_{\mathrm{patch}}+n_{\sigma}\sqrt{V_{\mathrm{patch}}}\penalty\ ,

with
n_{\sigma}
chosen to be 5, based on rate considerations.
The typical value for
E_{\textrm{T}}^{\mathrm{thres}}
after the L1 requirement is
30\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
.

With the threshold determined, patches are categorized as high-
E_{\text{T}}
or low-
E_{\text{T}}
if they have transverse
energy above or below
E_{\textrm{T}}^{\mathrm{thres}}
, respectively.
Any event that has no patch above threshold
has
pufit

E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}{}
set to zero and is rejected by the trigger. Approximately 97% of background events
that pass the
L1XE50
trigger have at least one hard-scatter patch.

Stage 2: performing the fit

The fit of the pile-up contribution to each of the high-
E_{\mathrm{T}}
patches
is performed by minimizing the
\chi^{2}
function

\chi^{2}({\mathcal{E}}_{{\textrm{T}}_{1}},\ldots,{\mathcal{E}}_{{\textrm{T}}_{m}})=\Delta^{T}V^{-1}\Delta\penalty\ .

In this expression
V
is the associated covariance matrix, and
the variables
{\mathcal{E}_{{\textrm{T}}_{k}}}
are the pile-up contributions to the transverse
energies of the
m
high-
E_{\text{T}}
patches.
The values of
{\mathcal{E}_{{\textrm{T}}_{k}}}
are determined by minimizing this
\chi^{2}
function.
The
(m+2)
-dimensional vector
\Delta
is given by

\Delta=\left(\begin{aligned} \sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}E_{x_{i}}&+\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mathcal{E}}_{{\textrm{T}}_{k}}\cos\phi_{k}\\
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}E_{y_{i}}&+\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mathcal{E}}_{{\textrm{T}}_{k}}\sin\phi_{k}\\
\left(A_{1}/A_{\mathrm{low}}\times\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}E_{{\textrm{T}}_{i}}\right)&-{\mathcal{E}}_{{\textrm{T}}_{1}}\\
&\vdots\\
\left(A_{m}/A_{\mathrm{low}}\times\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}E_{{\textrm{T}}_{i}}\right)&-{\mathcal{E}}_{{\textrm{T}}_{m}}\end{aligned}\right)

where the
A_{k}
are the areas of each of the
m
high-
E_{\text{T}}
patches,
and
A_{\mathrm{low}}
is the summed area of the low-
E_{\text{T}}
patches. The first two lines
impose the transverse momentum conservation constraints
\sum E_{x}=0
and
\sum E_{y}=0
on the pile-up
contributions. On each of those two lines, the first sum (for which
N_{\mathrm{low}}=N-m
)
runs over the low-
E_{\text{T}}
patches, and the
second over the estimated contributions of pile-up in the high-
E_{\text{T}}
patches.
The quantities

\cos\phi_{k}
and
\sin\phi_{k}
are given by
E_{x_{k}}/E_{{\textrm{T}}_{k}}
and

E_{y_{k}}/E_{{\textrm{T}}_{k}}
, respectively.
Each of the remaining
m
lines corresponds to one of the high-
E_{\text{T}}
patches, and penalizes any difference between the to-be-fitted pile-up estimate in that patch
{\mathcal{E}_{{\textrm{T}}_{k}}}

and the amount of pile-up transverse energy that would be expected in it, based
on the event-wide average transverse energy density
(as calculated from the low-
E_{\text{T}}
patches).
The event-wide transverse energy is given by the term

A_{k}/A_{\mathrm{low}}\times\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}E_{{\textrm{T}}_{i}}
,
where the ratio
\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}E_{{\textrm{T}}_{i}}\right)/A_{\mathrm{low}}
is
the average energy density from the low-
E_{\mathrm{T}}
patches.

For the determination of the covariance matrix, the fluctuations
of all of the low-
E_{\mathrm{T}}
patches are calculated from the contribution to the calorimeter energies due to detector resolution.
The uncertainty in each measured patch transverse energy
E_{{\textrm{T}}_{i}}
is taken as

\sigma_{i}^{2}=r_{0}^{2}+r^{2}E_{{\textrm{T}}_{i}}\penalty\ ,

where
r
is the resolution scale, set to
0.5\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}^{1/2}
and determined from calorimeter energy resolutions.
The
r_{0}
term is a resolution floor (
0.05\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
), introduced to avoid numerical problems for
events where most of the patches have no deposited
E_{\text{T}}
.
The patch sample variance
V_{\mathrm{patch}}
calculated
above is used for the lower
m
diagonal elements.
The full covariance matrix is

V=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}V_{11}&V_{12}&0&0&\ldots&0\\
V_{12}&V_{22}&0&0&\ldots&0\\
0&0&sV_{\mathrm{patch}}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&0&0&\ddots&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&\ldots&sV_{\mathrm{patch}}\\
\end{array}\right)

where the upper
2\times 2
block is given by

V_{\mathrm{cov}}\equiv\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}V_{11}&V_{12}\\
V_{12}&V_{22}\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{i}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}\sigma_{i}^{2}\cos^{2}\phi_{i}&\sum_{i}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}\sigma_{i}^{2}\cos\phi_{i}\sin\phi_{i}\\
\sum_{i}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}\sigma_{i}^{2}\cos\phi_{i}\sin\phi_{i}&\sum_{i}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}\sigma_{i}^{2}\sin^{2}\phi_{i}\\
\end{array}\right)\penalty\ ,

and
s=1
is determined by optimizing the fit.

The minimization
of the
\chi^{2}
function with respect to the
{\mathcal{E}}_{{\textrm{T}}_{k}}

is performed analytically,
avoiding computationally expensive numerical minimization.
The solution involves solving an
m\times m
linear system, where
m
, the number of above-threshold patches, is typically 4 or 5.

Minimizing the function gives

0=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\chi^{2}}{\partial{\mathcal{E}_{\textrm{T}}}_{k}}=(\cos\phi_{k},\,\sin\phi_{k})V^{-1}_{\mathrm{cov}}\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}E_{x_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{T}}_{j}}\cos\phi_{j}\\
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}E_{{y}_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{T}}_{j}}\sin\phi_{j}\\
\end{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{sV_{\mathrm{patch}}}\left(\left(\frac{A_{k}}{A_{\mathrm{low}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}{E_{\mathrm{T}}}_{i}\right)-{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}}}_{k}\right)\,.

The solution to this equation is given by the matrix equation

\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{T}}_{k}}=\left[X^{-1}\right]_{ki}c_{i}\penalty\ ,

where the
m\times m
matrix

X_{ik}=(\cos\phi_{i},\,\sin\phi_{i})V^{-1}_{\mathrm{cov}}\begin{pmatrix}\cos\phi_{k}\\
\sin\phi_{k}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{\delta_{ik}}{sV_{\mathrm{patch}}}

and

c_{i}=\frac{A_{i}}{A_{\mathrm{low}}}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}E_{{\mathrm{T}}_{j}}}{sV_{\mathrm{patch}}}-(\cos\phi_{i},\,\sin\phi_{i})V^{-1}_{\mathrm{cov}}\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}E_{x_{j}}\\
\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\mathrm{low}}}E_{y_{j}}\end{pmatrix}\,.

The
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
for the event is then
calculated from the
m
high-
E_{\text{T}}
patches and the fit results for the pile-up contribution to
the energy in these patches, where now

\displaystyle E_{x}^{\text{miss}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(E_{x_{k}}-{\mathcal{E}}_{{\textrm{T}}_{k}}\cos\phi_{k}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle E_{y}^{\text{miss}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(E_{y_{k}}-{\mathcal{E}}_{{\textrm{T}}_{k}}\sin\phi_{k}\right)\ ,

and finally
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}{}=\sqrt{(E_{x}^{\text{miss}})^{2}+(E_{y}^{\text{miss}})^{2}}
.

Appendix B
Details of the offline reconstruction algorithms

In the offline analyses, tracks with
p_{\text{T}}>400\,\text{Me\kern-1.00006ptV}
are reconstructed to identify a common vertex
[
42
]
and
an event is required to have at least one such vertex.
If more than one vertex is found in the event, the primary vertex is defined
as the one with the largest
\sum_{t}p_{\text{T},t}^{2}
,
where the sum runs over all tracks associated with the vertex.

Electrons are reconstructed by matching tracks that originate from the primary vertex
with clusters of energy deposits in the calorimeter
[
43
]
. They are required to
have
p_{\text{T}}>25\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and
|\eta|<2.47
. To suppress electrons that originate from the hadronic showers of jets,
the electron is also required to be isolated using information from the calorimeter and tracks within a cone of size
\Delta R=0.2
around the electron direction.
The total efficiency of electron reconstruction and isolation is 85%
for an electron with
p_{\text{T}}\approx 30\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
.

Muons are reconstructed by matching tracks from the muon spectrometer with inner-detector tracks that are associated with the primary
vertex
[
44
]
. Muons are required to have
p_{\text{T}}>25\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and
|\eta|<2.47
, and they
are required to be isolated. For the isolation working point used, the efficiency for a muon with
p_{\text{T}}
in the range 20–100
GeV
is 96%.

Jets are reconstructed starting with information from the calorimeter.
Cells are grouped into topological clusters
[
13
]
,
described in Section
3.3
,
which in turn are used to reconstruct jets using the anti-
k_{t}
algorithm
with a radius parameter of
R
= 0.4. Jets are required to have
p_{\text{T}}>20\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and

|\eta|<4.5
. To suppress jets that come from pile-up, those that have
|\eta|<2.4
are
required to exceed the jet vertex tagger (JVT) threshold
[
25
]
.
The JVT uses tracks and vertices to assign a likelihood for a jet to be associated with the
primary vertex.
Jets that fail the JVT requirement are removed, as they are likely to be due to pile-up.
The efficiency to select a jet from a signal process is 92%, while jets originating from
pile-up collisions are rejected 99% of the time in the
p_{\text{T}}
range 20–50
GeV
.
Each jet with

|\eta|<2.5
is evaluated for the likelihood that it originated from a

b
-hadron
[
45
]
.
The efficiency for tagging a jet containing a
b
-hadron is 77%
for jets originating from
t\bar{t}
events
[
46
]
.

When selecting events based on the number of jets and leptons (electrons or muons), it is
necessary to resolve the ambiguity for cases where a reconstructed jet and a lepton
both result from the same detector signals.
To remove such ambiguities, a sequential overlap removal procedure is defined.
If the jet is within a cone of size
\Delta R
= 0.2 around any electron,
the jet is removed. If a jet is found within a conical annulus defined by
0.2<\Delta R<0.4
, then the electron is removed.
If there is a muon within
\Delta R=0.4
of a jet, and the jet has at least three charged tracks with
p_{\text{T}}>500\,\text{Me\kern-1.00006ptV}
,
then the muon is removed; otherwise the jet is removed.

Appendix C
The
cell

E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
background distribution model

The details of the background
cell

E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
distribution model discussed in Section
5.1
are as follows.
The model has two components. The first covers the bulk of the distribution, which is assumed to originate from resolution effects.
The second component, which dominates in the tail, comes from rare occurrences which scale linearly with the luminosity,
and hence, for fixed beam parameters, linearly with the instantaneous average number
\mu
of interactions per bunch crossing.

Component 1:
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
resolution-dependent bulk distribution

The bulk resolution effects are assumed to depend on the total transverse
energy deposited in the calorimeter,
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
.
It is assumed that the two perpendicular components of
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
are independently identically Gaussian distributed with a width

\sigma(\Sigma E_{\text{T}})
that depends
on
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
,
such that events with a given
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
will obey a Rayleigh distribution
in
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
,

R\left(E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}|\sigma(\Sigma E_{\text{T}})\right)=(E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}/\sigma^{2})\exp(-[E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}]^{2}/2\sigma^{2}).

In turn the probability density function of
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
is assumed to be a function

P(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\mu,\vec{\gamma})
, where
\mu
is the instantaneous average number
of interactions per bunch crossing and

\vec{\gamma}
is used to parameterize any other dependencies.
For a given
\mu
,
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
is distributed according to the probability density function

P_{\textrm{res}}(E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}|\mu)=\int_{0}^{\infty}R(E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}|\sigma(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}))\,P(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\mu,\vec{\gamma})\penalty\ {\mathrm{d}}(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}).

(2)

((a))

((b))

Figure 13:
Data and fits to data for
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
model.
A comparison of the measured cell-algorithm
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
distribution with
a Rayleigh distribution fit, for a narrow range of pile-up centred on
\langle\mu\rangle\approx 50
. The black histogram is the distribution from the unbiased data. The red curve is the result of a fit to a Rayleigh distribution.
Values of
\sigma
are shown from Rayleigh fits to

E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
distributions for 6 values of
\langle\mu\rangle
and 50 values of
\sqrt{\Sigma E_{\text{T}}}
per
\langle\mu\rangle
value, as well as the data from which the fits are obtained. Also shown are straight-line fits to the 50
\sqrt{\Sigma E_{\text{T}}}
bins for each
\langle\mu\rangle
.

In order to sample from the distribution
P_{\textrm{res}}(E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}|\mu)
,
expressions for both
\sigma(\Sigma E_{\text{T}})
and
P(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\mu,\vec{\gamma})
are needed.

Fits to find
\sigma(\Sigma E_{\text{T}})
are discussed first. Figure
13(a)
shows an example
cell

E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
distribution for
a narrow range around
\langle\mu\rangle\approx 50
and
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}\approx 400\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
.
The plot also shows a Rayleigh fit to the
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
distribution; the fit characterizes
it well except at the highest
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
values.
The points in Figure
13(b)
show the values of
\sigma
obtained from
fits to the
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
distribution for
6 values of
\langle\mu\rangle
and 50 bins of
(\Sigma E_{\text{T}})^{1/2}
for each
\langle\mu\rangle
.
Although some
\langle\mu\rangle
dependence is visible, the model provides
a reasonable description of the data
using a parameterization of the resolution given by
\sigma(\Sigma E_{\text{T}})=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\sqrt{\Sigma E_{\text{T}}}

with
\alpha_{1}=3\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and
\alpha_{2}=0.465\,{\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}^{1/2}
.
Plots like those in Figure
13(b)
show that this linear fit is a good model for the bulk of events and performs poorly only in ranges of the
total transverse energy where the Rayleigh fits do not provide a good description of the
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
.

To find the
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
probability density function
P(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\mu,\vec{\gamma})
,
one may first consider the distribution

S_{1}(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\vec{\gamma})
of the
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
for a single proton–proton interaction.
The corresponding distribution
S_{n}(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\vec{\gamma})
for
n
simultaneous proton–proton interactions
is given by the
n
-fold convolution of
S_{1}(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\vec{\gamma})
.
The values of
n
that are sampled for any
\mu
are assumed to be Poisson distributed with mean
\mu
,
so that the resulting distribution

P(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\mu,\vec{\gamma})
is given by a Poisson-weighted sum of terms, each term being the product
of the Poisson probability for
n
interactions given
\mu
with the corresponding
S_{n}(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\vec{\gamma})
:

P(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\mu,\vec{\gamma})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{Poi}(n|\mu)S_{n}(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\vec{\gamma})\penalty\ .

In the absence of data taken at low
\langle\mu\rangle
under the same beam conditions,

S_{1}(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\vec{\gamma})
cannot be determined directly.
Instead, various models of
S_{1}(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\vec{\gamma})
are explored
by varying their parameters until the calculation gives a reasonable match to
the measured high-luminosity
P(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\mu,\vec{\gamma})
distributions.
It is found that a single-interaction
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
distribution of the form

S_{1}(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\vec{\gamma})=\gamma_{3}\gamma_{1}\exp(-\gamma_{1}\Sigma^{\prime})+(1-\gamma_{3})\gamma_{2}\exp(-\gamma_{2}\Sigma^{\prime})

gives a result that matches the data well for
\mu
of about 20 or greater. The parameters of this expression are
\gamma_{1}=$0.070\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
,
\gamma_{2}=$0.009\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
,
\gamma_{3}=0.995
, and for the

\mu
-dependent substitution
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma E_{\text{T}}-(\gamma_{4}+\gamma_{5}\times\mu)

with
\gamma_{4}=$25\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
and
\gamma_{5}=$-5\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
.
Figure
14
shows an example of the
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
modelling for
\langle\mu\rangle\approx 50
.

The
n
-fold convolution can be carried out analytically only for a limited number of functions.
For example, the single-interaction
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}
distribution for the LHC Run 1 was approximated by a single exponential function,
and the convolution was then performed as described in Ref.
[
47
]
.
For the more complicated functions used in the present calculation, the convolution and Poisson sum
is computed using a fast Fourier transform
and the method described in Refs.
[
48
]
and
[
49
]
.

The Fourier transform
\widetilde{P}(\omega|\mu,\vec{\gamma})

of the weighted convolution sum
P(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\mu,\vec{\gamma})

is rewritten as the infinite sum of Fourier transforms
over
S_{1}(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\vec{\gamma})
, denoted by
\widetilde{S_{1}}(\omega|\vec{\gamma})
.
This facilitates calculation, as the infinite sum can be performed using the Fourier transform:

\begin{split}\widetilde{P}(\omega|\mu,\vec{\gamma})&=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{Poi}(n|\mu)\widetilde{S_{n}}(\omega|\vec{\gamma})\\
&=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{Poi}(n|\mu)(2\pi)^{n-1}(\widetilde{S_{1}}(\omega|\vec{\gamma}))^{n}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathrm{e}^{-\mu}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(2\pi)^{n}\mu^{n}(\widetilde{S_{1}}(\omega|\vec{\gamma}))^{n}/n!\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathrm{e}^{-\mu}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}[2\pi\mu\widetilde{S_{1}}(\omega|\vec{\gamma})]^{n}/n!-1\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathrm{e}^{-\mu}\left(\exp\left(2\pi\mu\widetilde{S_{1}}(\omega|\vec{\gamma})\right)-1\right).\end{split}

(3)

In principle, one could also take a logarithm of the exponential term in the final expression above to directly
solve for
\widetilde{S_{1}}(\omega|\vec{\gamma})

from
\widetilde{P}(\omega|\mu,\vec{\gamma})
.
However, the uncertainties in the
P(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\mu,\vec{\gamma})

distribution are amplified in this process, making it impossible in practice to obtain useful results in this way.

Figure 14:

The probability density function
P(\Sigma E_{\text{T}}|\mu,\vec{\gamma})

described in the text is shown
and compared with data recorded using a zero bias trigger.
The model and the data both correspond to pile-up of
\langle\mu\rangle\approx 50
.

Component 2: Luminosity-dependent tail from rare events

The Rayleigh distribution resolution model described above is found to describe the observed
cell

E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
distribution well for
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
up to about 50
GeV
, but not beyond.
A second component, which contributes more at larger
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}

values, is assumed to come from a combination of events with rare calorimeter measurement fluctuations,
for example very poor measurement of a single jet in a two-jet event, and from events containing non-interacting particles,
such as those in which semileptonic
b
-hadron decays produce neutrinos.
The probability of such an event is expected to scale linearly with luminosity,
and hence for fixed bunch-crossing rate, to scale linearly with
\mu
.
Indeed, a Fréchet function that is linearly dependent on
\mu
,

\mu F(E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}|m,\alpha,s)=\mu A\frac{\alpha}{s}\left(\frac{E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}-m}{s}\right)^{-1-\alpha}\exp\left[{-\left(\frac{E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}-m}{s}\right)^{-\alpha}}\right]

(4)

is found to describe the high
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
distribution,
with parameters
\alpha=3.54
,
s=8\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
, and
m=40\,\text{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
, and
with the probability per single
pp
interaction of such a tail event being
A=10^{-5}
.

Full
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
distribution

The complete model
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
distribution is then obtained by vector addition of the two
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
sources.
A random azimuthal angle
\phi_{\text{rand}}
is assumed between the two vectors and the magnitude of the
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
is sampled from two sources. These are
(i) the
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
distribution in Eq. (
2
) from the resolution function
of the total transverse energy in Eq. (
3
), and
(ii) a tail modelled with the Fréchet distribution, depending linearly upon
\mu
, in Eq. (
4
).
The combination yields

\text{Event\ }E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}=\big|E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}(\text{resolution})\penalty\ \hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathrm{T}}(\phi=0)+E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}(\text{tail})\penalty\ \hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathrm{T}}(\phi=\phi_{\text{rand}})\big|.

It is the distribution of this quantity that is compared with data in Section
5.1
.
